# ON MERCURY.

<meta>catchword: to the la-</meta>

...I do not leave those who accompany me; with them I stay, with them I perish. I am an immortal body: I die indeed when I am killed, but I rise again for judgment before a wise judge. <note>Mercury is describing its chemical volatility and resilience; it appears to "die" (transform or fix) during a reaction but "rises" (returns to its pure, volatile state) when tested or heated by a "wise judge" (the skilled alchemist).</note>

**ALCHEMIST.** So, are you the **Philosophers’ Stone**?

**MERCURY.** My mother is such a thing, and from her one thing is born through art; but my brother, who lives in the citadel, holds in his will what the Philosopher desires. <note>In alchemical allegory, the "mother" often represents the raw mineral matter or *Prima Materia*. The "brother in the citadel" usually symbolizes Gold or "Fixed Sulfur," the stable principle that the alchemist seeks to unite with Mercury.</note>

**ALCHEMIST.** Are you an old man?

**MERCURY.** My mother gave birth to me, but I am older than my mother. <note>This is a classic alchemical paradox: the "spirit" of the metal is considered eternal and primordial, even if its physical form was produced later in time.</note>

**ALCHEMIST.** What Devil can understand you, since you do not answer me to the point? You are always speaking in riddles. Tell me, are you the small fountain <note>original Latin: "fontina"</note> about which <term>Bernard, Count of Trevisan</term> wrote? <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490) was a famous Italian alchemist. He wrote a celebrated allegory about a King bathing in a magic fountain, which represented the process of metallic dissolution and purification.</note>

**MERCURY.** I am not the fountain, but I am the water; the fountain surrounded me.

**ALCHEMIST.** Is gold dissolved in you, since you are water?

**MERCURY.** Whatever is with me I love as a friend; and whatever is born with me, I give it nourishment; and whatever is naked, I cover with my wings. <note>Mercury’s "wings" are a standard symbol for its volatility (its tendency to evaporate). This passage refers to Mercury's ability to form amalgams—it "nourishes" and "covers" other metals by dissolving them into itself.</note>

**ALCHEMIST.** I see that it is impossible to speak with you; I ask one thing, you say another. If you do not wish to answer properly, I will certainly go with you <insert>now to the labor</insert>...

<summary>In this dialogue, Mercury continues to frustrate the Alchemist with riddles regarding its origin, its relationship to the "Philosophers' Stone," and its role as the transformative solvent described by Bernard of Trevisan.</summary>

...to the labors. *Merc.* O Lord, I beg

spirit of fire love each other, and 
one accompanies the other as much as it can. <note>This refers to the alchemical property of volatility; mercury "loves" heat because heat allows it to rise as vapor, but it appears to flee because it evaporates when the heat becomes too intense.</note> *Alch.* And 
where are you accustomed to go, when 
you ascend with the fire? <note>"Ascending with fire" describes the process of sublimation or distillation, where the substance turns to vapor and rises in the flask.</note> *Merc.* Know this: every 
traveler always tends toward his own homeland, 
and when he comes to that place from 
which he departed, he rests, and he always 
returns wiser than when he left. <note>Mercury is describing the cycle of distillation: it leaves the liquid (the "homeland"), becomes vapor, and then condenses back into liquid, having been purified or "made wiser" by the process.</note> *Alch.* Do you return 
again sometime? *Merc.* I return, 
but in another form. *Alch.* I do not understand 
what this is, nor anything about the fire. 
*Merc.* If anyone knew the fire of my 
heart, he would see that fire (the proper heat) <note>original: "calorem debitum" – the specific, tempered heat required for alchemical success, as opposed to a destructive, raging flame.</note> 
is my food; and the longer the 
spirit of my heart eats the fire, 
the fatter it will be; its death is 
afterwards the life of all 
things that are in this kingdom 
where I am. <note>This paradox suggests that as Mercury "consumes" heat, it becomes more potent ("fatter"). When it is finally "killed" or fixed into a solid state, it becomes the catalyst that gives "life" (transmutation) to other metals.</note> *Alch.* Are 
you great? *Merc.* You have me as an 
example: out of a thousand little drops <note>original: "guttulis"</note> 
I will be one; out of one, I give

->## ON MERCURY.<-

as my body is before your eyes: If you know how to play with me, you can divide me as much as you wish, and I will be one again; <note>Mercury, or quicksilver, has the unique physical property of breaking into many tiny droplets when poured and then immediately merging back into a single liquid mass when they touch.</note> what then is my spirit (my heart) within, which always brings forth many thousands from the smallest part?

*Alchemist.* And how then must one act with you, so that you are thus?

*Mercury.* I am fire within, fire is my food; but the life of fire is air, and without air, fire is extinguished: <note>This reflects the ancient and medieval understanding of combustion—that fire "eats" fuel and requires "air" (oxygen) to live.</note> fire prevails over air, therefore I do not rest, nor can raw air constrain me. Add air to air, so that both are one and hold weight; join them with hot fire, and give them to the seasons to guard. <note>Mercury is describing the alchemical process of "digestion" or "maturation," where the substance is kept at a steady heat for a long period (seasons) in a sealed vessel.</note>

*Alchemist.* What will happen afterward?

*Mercury.* The superfluous shall be removed, you shall burn the residue with fire, place it in water, cook it afterward, and when cooked, you shall give it as medicine to the sick.

*Alchemist.* You tell me nothing in response to my questions. I see that you wish to elude me with nothing but parables. Wife, bring the pig dung! <note>Pig dung was often used in alchemy as a source of gentle, steady heat. A vessel would be buried in the fermenting manure to maintain a constant "warmth of the womb" for months.</note> I will treat this Mercury in new ways, until he tells me how the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapis Philosophicus" — the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> is to be made from him. Mercury, having heard these things...

against my will you proceed with my sons: where you ought to <term>revivify</term> <note>original: *reuiuificare*; to bring a substance back to a "living" or reactive state.</note>, you kill; where you should fix, you <term>sublime</term> <note>original: *sublimas*; the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, often used for purification.</note>; where you should <term>calcine</term> <note>original: *calcinare*; reducing a metal to a powder through high heat.</note>, you <term>distill</term>; especially this most obedient son of mine, **Mercury**, whom you torture with so many corrosive waters and so many poisonous things. *Alchemist.* Then I shall proceed with him gently by means of <term>digestion</term> <note>original: *digestio*; a process of gentle, sustained heating, mimicking the slow "cooking" of materials within the earth or a stomach.</note> alone. it is nothing, and its number is a whole. <note>In alchemical philosophy, Mercury is often described as the "Materia Prima" or first matter—it is nothing specific yet contains the potential for everything.</note> In it are the four Elements, yet it is not itself an Element. It is a Spirit, yet it possesses a body. It is a Man, and yet it plays the part of a woman <note>original: "vices fœminæ agit". This refers to the hermaphroditic or dual nature of Mercury in alchemy, representing the union of opposites.</note>. It is a boy, and yet it carries the weapons of a man. It is an animal, and yet it has the wings of a bird. It is a poison, and yet it cures leprosy <note>In alchemy, "leprosy" often refers to the "sickness" or impurity of base metals that need to be "cured" into gold.</note>. It is earth, and yet it is sown. It is air, and it lives by water.

*Alchemist.* Now I see that I know nothing, but I do not dare to say so; for I would lose my good reputation, and my neighbor would no longer spend money on me if he knew that I know nothing. However, I will still say that I know for certain, otherwise no one would give me even bread; for many of them hope for many good things from me.

NATURE. Even if you drag this out for a long time, what will happen afterward? Meanwhile, any of your neighbors will demand back their expenses from you.

*Alchemist.* I will feed them all with hope as long as I can.

NATURE. And finally, what in the end?

*Alchemist.* I will try various works in secret, and if it succeeds—

# THE NEW CHEMICAL LIGHT
<note>original: "Novi Luminis Chymici". This title refers to the influential work by Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a Polish alchemist. The "New Light" suggests a clearer, more modern explanation of alchemical secrets compared to the obscure writings of the Middle Ages.</note>

### *The second part.*

## CONCERNING <term>SULFUR</term>: THE OTHER PRINCIPLE OF NATURE.
<note>In the "Tria Prima" or three-principle theory of alchemy, <term>Sulfur</term> (the soul) was paired with Mercury (the spirit) and Salt (the body). Sulfur represents the active, masculine, combustible, and transformative power within matter.</note>

<summary>This is the internal title page for the second part of Michael Sendivogius's alchemical treatise, shifting the focus from the study of Mercury to the study of Sulfur as a primary building block of nature.</summary>

<keywords>New Chemical Light, Sulfur, Alchemy, Second Principle, Michael Sendivogius, Tria Prima, Natural Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page follows the internal title page for the second part of Michael Sendivogius's work. It serves as the author's preface to the discourse on Sulfur, establishing the author's persona and his reasons for writing.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut headpiece featuring symmetrical floral and vine scrollwork.</image-desc>

# PREFACE.

Since I am not permitted, **Kind Reader** <note>original: "Lector beneuole". A standard, polite Latin address to the audience in 17th-century literature.</note>, to write more openly than the other **Ancient Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists and natural philosophers of the past who were believed to have hidden their secrets in allegorical language.</note> have written, perhaps you will not be satisfied with my writings. This is especially likely since you already have so many other books by these Philosophers at hand. But believe me, I have no real need to compose books, for I seek neither profit nor vain ostentation from them; for that reason, I do not even wish to make my identity known. <note>Sendivogius often published under the pseudonym "Cosmopolita" to maintain the secrecy and safety required of an alchemist in an era when they were often sought after by greedy rulers.</note>

The things I have already made public for your benefit seemed to me to be more than enough. I had intended to set aside the remaining matters for the *Harmony* <note>original: "Harmoniam". This likely refers to a larger, more comprehensive work or a specific philosophical system the author was developing to reconcile the laws of nature.</note>, where I have proposed many things extensively concerning natural matters. However, at the urging of friends, I felt compelled to write this treatise on **Sulfur** as well. I do not know if it is necessary to add anything further to my previous writings here; indeed, if the works of so many other Philosophers have not satisfied you, this one will not satisfy you either; And

<summary>In this preface, the author justifies his decision to write on Sulfur despite the existing abundance of alchemical literature, while maintaining his commitment to traditional secrecy and personal anonymity.</summary>

<keywords>Preface, Michael Sendivogius, Alchemy, Sulfur, Secrecy, Ancient Philosophers, Harmony, Anonymity</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from the previous page regarding the superfluity of many alchemical books and the importance of observing nature directly. It maintains the same tone of cautious guidance for the "benevolent reader."</meta>

-># PREFACE.<-

especially if the daily operation of nature is not a sufficient example for you, no other examples will help: for if you were to consider with mature judgment how nature operates, you would have no need for so many volumes of the **Philosophers**, since in my judgment it is better to learn from the master, **Nature** herself, than from the disciples. You have enough in the preface of the little book *The Twelve Treatises* <note>original: *XII. Tractatuum*; likely referring to Michael Sendivogius's *Novum Lumen Chymicum*, which consists of twelve treatises</note>, as well as in the first treatise there, [explaining] that in this **art** <term>art|The "Great Art" (Ars Magna) of alchemy, specifically the search for the Philosopher's Stone</term> so many and such great books are found that they now hinder seekers more than they help. And so it seems to be, since the writings of the **Philosophers** have grown from such a small scroll of **Hermes** <note>A reference to the *Emerald Tablet* of Hermes Trismegistus, the brief foundational text of Hermetic philosophy</note> into such a great and most erroneous labyrinth, and daily decline toward an **Eclipse** <note>original: *Eclypsim*; the author suggests the light of true knowledge is being obscured by over-complicated writings</note>. And I believe this has been done by none but envious experts, since an ignorant person would not know what ought to be added or subtracted, unless perhaps the author's handwriting could not be easily read. If in any science it helps or harms much if a single word is missing or added, it is especially so in this one.

For example: it is written in one place: *afterwards you should mix these waters together*,

<summary>The author argues that observing nature is superior to reading the confusing "labyrinth" of alchemical books, which he claims have been intentionally obscured by "envious" experts.</summary>
<keywords>Nature as teacher, Twelve Treatises, Hermes Trismegistus, alchemical secrecy, corruption of texts, the Art of alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's warning about how a single word—like the addition of "not"—can completely alter the meaning of alchemical texts. It transitions into advice on how a student should approach these difficult writings by looking to nature as the ultimate guide.</meta>

-># PREFACE.<-

another added *not*; he added very little, yet nonetheless made the whole chapter say the opposite. Yet let the diligent student know that even from poisonous herbs, the bee collects honey; for if he directs his reading toward the **possibility of nature** <note>A central alchemical concept: one should only believe what is physically possible according to the laws of nature</note>, he will easily overcome **Sophisms** <term>sophisms|In this context, deceptive or intentionally misleading arguments placed in texts to test the reader's wisdom</term>, and yet he should not stop reading, because "one book opens another" <note>original: *liber librum aperit*; a common alchemical maxim suggesting that the secrets of one author are clarified by the writings of another</note>.

But I have understood it this way: that the books of **Geber** <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in alchemy whose works were known for being notoriously difficult to decipher—hence the word "gibberish"</note>, the **Philosophers**, and—who knows?—perhaps those of other authors as well, have been enchanted with the greatest "poisonous spells" <note>original: *veneficiis*; here used metaphorically to mean the texts are "charmed" or intentionally obscured to mislead the unworthy</note>, so that they can and should in no way be understood except upon the thousandth reading, and even then only by an ingenious reader; fools are plainly excluded from reading them. 

Indeed, there are many who interpret him, as they do other authors, but I see that the explanation is often more difficult than the text itself. My advice is that you stay with the text, and whatever you read, apply it all to the **possibility of nature**, and first of all, investigate what nature actually is. Everyone writes that the [Great] Work is a cheap, easy, and common thing, and this is indeed true, but it should have been added: "to those who know." One who knows recognizes it even among dung, whereas the ignorant person even...

<meta>catchword: rale</meta>

<summary>The author warns that alchemical texts are intentionally obscured by "poisonous" complexity and "sophisms," specifically citing Geber as a difficult author. He advises readers to focus on the "possibility of nature" and to seek the common, "cheap" matter of the Stone which the wise can find even in filth.</summary>

<keywords>Geber, sophisms, possibility of nature, alchemical study, interpretation of texts, common matter, bee and honey metaphor</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 116 (represented in the previous context), which introduced the chapter "ON SULFUR" and quoted Geber regarding Sulfur's status as a "light from light." # ON SULFUR.

First, I must describe the origin of the principles, especially since Sulfur was regarded in ancient times as the principal of all Principles. Few have shown until now where these Principles originate, and it is difficult to judge any of them—just as it is with any other thing—if its origin and generation are unknown; for what can a blind man say about color? Therefore, what our ancestors omitted, we have proposed to fulfill in this Treatise.

According to the ancient Philosophers, there are two Principles of things, especially of Metals: Sulfur and Mercury. However, according to the Moderns <note>original: "Neotericos"; likely referring to the Paracelsian school of alchemy which added Salt to the traditional duo of Sulfur and Mercury.</note>, there are three: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury. The origin of these Principles is the four Elements, and it is with their origin that we must primarily begin. Therefore, let the students of this Holy Science <note>original: "S. Scientiæ," short for Sacræ Scientiæ.</note> know that there are four Elements: and each of these four has at its center another Element, from which it is "elementated" <term>To be "elementated" (elementatur) refers to the alchemical process where a substance derives its inner nature or power from a core element acting upon it.</term>. These are the four Pillars of the World <note>original: "Statuæ Mundi"; literally "Statues of the World," signifying the foundational supports of physical reality.</note>, separated from Chaos during the Creation of the World by Divine Wisdom.

<summary>This page explains the origins of the alchemical Principles (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury), tracing them back to the four elements created by Divine Wisdom. It highlights the author's intent to clarify the foundations of these substances, which he argues were overlooked by previous generations.</summary>
<keywords>Sulfur, Mercury, Salt, Three Principles, Four Elements, Creation, Chaos, Alchemical Theory</keywords>

Here, to our purpose: first, regarding the closest Element, Earth.

# ON THE ELEMENT OF EARTH

Earth is an element of considerable worth in its quality and dignity. Within this Element, the other three—and especially Fire—find their rest. It is the most excellent Element for both concealing and revealing the things entrusted to it. It is coarse and porous; heavy in regard to its smallness, yet light in regard to its true nature. <note>The author suggests that while earth feels heavy because it is dense and compact, its essential elemental nature is "light" or subtle when viewed through a philosophical lens.</note> It is also the Center of the World, as well as the center of the other Elements. Through its center passes the Axis of the World <note>The *Axis Mundi*, an imaginary line connecting the celestial poles around which the heavens were thought to rotate in the geocentric model.</note> from both Poles. It is porous, as we have said, like a sponge, which produces nothing of its own accord. Rather, it receives everything that the other three elements drip and cast into it,

# ON THE ELEMENT OF WATER.

Water is the heaviest **Element** <note>original: "Elementum"</note>, full of oily **phlegm** <note>original: "phlegmatis"; in alchemy, "phlegm" usually refers to a watery, tasteless, and inert substance, but here it implies a specific internal moisture or substance.</note>, and it is the most worthy Element in its quality: volatile on the outside, but truly **fixed** <note>original: "fixum"; referring to its internal stability or its capacity to be solidified into physical forms.</note> within. It is cold and moist, and is tempered by the **Air**. It is the **Sperm of the World** <note>original: "sperma Mundi"; the vehicle or medium that carries the vital life-force of the universe.</note>, in which the **seed** <note>original: "semen"</note> of all things is preserved; it is the guardian of the seed of every thing. 

It must be known, however, that the **seed** is one thing, and the **sperm** is another. The receptacle of the Sperm is the **Earth**, while the receptacle of the Seed is **Water**. Whatever the **Air** drips into the water—caused by the action of **Fire**—this the water extends to the earth. Sometimes the Sperm lacks sufficient seed, due to a deficiency of the heat that digests it: for the Sperm is always in abundance, waiting for the seed, which is carried into the **matrix** <note>original: "matricem"; the "womb" or site of generation within the earth or a vessel.</note> from the **imagination of fire** <note>original: "imaginatione ignis"; an alchemical concept suggesting that fire has a formative, "thinking" power that directs the creation of forms.</note> through the motion of the air. We will speak of these things more extensively later in the third of the Principles, in the <term>Treatise on Salt</term> <note>original: "Salis Tractatu"; Salt, along with Sulfur and Mercury, formed the "Tria Prima" or three primary substances of Paracelsian alchemy.</note>. It happens most often in Nature that the Sperm with suffi- <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "cienti" completes the word "sufficienti" or "sufficient."</note>

# ON SULFUR.

[the effi]cient seed enters the **matrix** <term>Matrix: Latin for "womb." In alchemical philosophy, this refers to the receptive environment—often Earth or Water—where the seeds of things are nurtured into existence.</term>; but if the matrix is ill-disposed, filled with **faulty sulfurs** <note>original: "peccantibus sulphuribus." This refers to "sinning" or "corrupt" sulfur—chemical impurities that prevent the proper "conception" or formation of minerals or life.</note>, or with phlegms, it does not conceive, nor does that happen which ought to. In this **Element** [Water] also, nothing is properly present, except what is usually found in **Sperma** <term>Sperma: The "seed-matter" or universal fluid that carries the specific "semen" or life-spark.</term>: it is delighted mostly by its own motion, which occurs through the air, and it is easily mixed with things because of its superficial, volatile body.

It is, as we have said, the receptacle of the **universal seed**: within it, the earth is easily purified and dissolved, and the air is congealed within it and joined to it. In its depths is the **Menstruum of the World** <term>Menstruum Mundi: Literally "The World's Monthly Blood." In alchemy, this is the "Universal Solvent" or the vital fluid of nature that dissolves, purifies, and nourishes all things.</term>, which the air, by penetrating it through the virtue of heat, draws out with it as a hot vapor. This vapor causes the natural generation of those things with which the earth is impregnated, just like a matrix; and when the matrix has received its due portion of seed, whatever is being formed now proceeds, and nature works without intermission until the very end.

But the residual moisture, or **Sperma**, falls to the side; as heat acts upon the earth, that rejected matter putrefies, and from it afterwards other things are generated—sometimes various **little beasts and little worms**. <note>The author is referring to the theory of "spontaneous generation," the historical belief that life (like maggots or insects) could arise from decaying matter.</note> Indeed, an artist of sagacious wit could, in this Ele[ment]...

# ON SULFUR.

F 2

[...fer]ment, just as from sperm, one may see the various miracles of Nature; but it would be necessary to receive that **Sperm** in which the **astral seed** <term>Semen Astrale: The "star-seed." In alchemical thought, this is a celestial influence or "blueprint" that descends from the stars to give specific form to matter.</term> is already imprinted <note>original: "imaginatum." This suggests the seed has been "imaged" or given a specific formative pattern by the cosmic imagination.</note>, of a certain weight. This is because Nature makes and produces pure things from the first **putrefaction** <term>Putrefactio: The stage of decay or breaking down. Alchemists believed that for a new life to begin, the old form must first rot and dissolve.</term>; but from a second putrefaction, she produces things much purer, much worthier, and more noble. 

You have an example of this in **living wood** <note>original: "ligno vegetabili."</note>: where in the first composition Nature makes wood, but when that wood is corrupted after maturity, it rots, and from it are generated worms and other small creatures having life and sight. It is evident that a **sensitive being** <note>A creature with senses and the power of motion, such as an insect or animal.</note> is always worthier than a **vegetable being** <note>A plant, which grows but does not perceive.</note>; for a much more subtle and purer matter is required for the organs of sensitive beings. 

But to the point. We say that this **Element** [Water] is the **Menstruum of the World** <term>Menstruum Mundi: "The World's Solvent." A vital, life-sustaining fluid that dissolves and nourishes all things, analogous to how blood sustains an embryo.</term>, and is divided in a threefold way: namely, the pure, the purer, and the purest. From its **purest substance** the heavens were created; the **purer** part was resolved into air; while the **pure** but simple and thick part remained in its own sphere. By Divine ordinance and the working of Nature, it guards every subtle thing and forms one globe together with the Earth. It also has its own cen- <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword indicates the next word is "centrum" or "center."</note>

<term>solvent</term> <note>original: "menstruum"</note> in the heart of the sea: it has one polar axis with the Earth, through which all the courses and springs of water go out, which afterwards grow into great rivers. By such outflows of water, the Earth is preserved from <note>The author suggests that without the constant circulation of water, the Earth would be consumed by its own internal heat or the heat of the sun.</note>combustion, and with this moistening, the **universal seed** <note>original: "semen universale." In alchemy, this refers to a fundamental life-force or formative essence that allows minerals, plants, and animals to grow.</note> is carried through the pores of the whole earth, which movement and heat cause.

It is evident, however, that all courses of water return to the heart of the sea, but where they go after that is not clear to everyone: for there are some who believe that all rivers, waters, and springs, which have their course into the sea, are generated from the stars; and because they know no other reason why the sea does not overflow from them, they say these waters are consumed in the heart of the sea.

But this has no place in Nature, as we have shown regarding rains. The stars indeed cause <note>That is, they exert influence or provide the "spark."</note>, but they do not generate; for nothing is generated except in its own likeness of the same species: but the stars are made of **fire** and **air**, how then shall they generate **waters**? And if it were so, that certain stars generated waters, then necessarily others would have generated earth, and still others the other **Elements**:

...of the table of divine wisdom <note>original Latin: "mensæ sapiẽtiæ diuinæ"</note>, that fire was kindled which afterwards distilled those most pure waters. But because that most pure fire has now occupied the firmament along with the throne of God Most High, the waters remained beneath it, and were condensed into the heavens under that fire. To ensure they were more strongly established, a fire—now coarser <note>The author suggests that fire exists in different states of purity, with the highest being the most "subtle" and the lower being "gross" or thick.</note> than the first—distilled them, driven always by that central fire which remained in the Sphere of fire <note>In the traditional Aristotelian and alchemical worldview, the "Sphere of Fire" was the highest elemental region, located just above the air and below the celestial spheres.</note> beneath the waters; and thus the waters were congealed and compressed into heavens between two fires. 

Yet that Central fire did not cease, but by distilling further, it also resolved other, less pure waters into air. This air also remained in its own Sphere beneath the Sphere of fire, and is surrounded by the element of fire as if by a most powerful foundation. Just as the waters of the heavens cannot pass beyond that super-celestial fire, so the element of fire cannot pass beyond the waters of the heavens, nor can the air pass beyond or be raised above the element of fire. Water, however, remained with the earth in a single globe, since it has no place in the air—except for that part which fire resolves <note>The author views evaporation as a "resolution" of water into air, a process of refinement driven by heat.</note> into air for the daily strengthening of this...

This operation happens from day to day, and thus this world is also strengthened day by day; it is meant to remain naturally incorruptible, except by the absolute will of the Most High Creator. 

This is because that central fire will not cease to be kindled by the universal motion and the influence of celestial virtues, nor will it cease to heat the waters. Likewise, the waters will not cease being resolved into air, nor will the air cease to compress the remainder of the waters against the earth. Thus, the air contains them in the center so that they cannot move from their central place. In this way, this world was naturally made and is maintained by the highest divine wisdom; following this example, it is necessary that all things in the world happen naturally. ...know that natural springs <note>original: *scaturigines*</note> and all the outlets of waters are not generated from the stars, but from the center of the sea, to which they return again, thus maintaining a continuous motion. For if this were not so, nothing at all would be produced either in the earth or upon the earth; indeed, the ruin of the world would follow.

But lest it be objected that all the waters in the sea are salty while the waters of springs are sweet: know the reason is that this water is distilled <note>The author views the earth as a giant laboratory where sand and stone act as a filter or "alchemical still" to purify seawater.</note> through the pores of the earth. Passing many miles through narrow places and through sands, it loses its saltiness and is sweetened; it was by this example that cisterns <note>Likely referring to sand-filtration systems used to create drinkable water.</note> were invented.

There are also in some places larger pores and passages <term>meatus|natural channels or conduits within the earth's crust</term> through which salty water breaks out, from which salt mines and salt springs are afterwards obtained, such as Halle in Germany <note>Halle has been a center of salt production since the Bronze Age due to its brine springs.</note>.

In some places, the water is even constricted by heat and the salt is left behind in the sands, while the water "sweats" into other pores, as at Wieliczka and Bochnia in Poland <note>Two of the oldest and most famous salt mines in Europe, in continuous operation from the 13th century until recently.</note>.

Likewise, when waters pass through hot sulfurous places that are continuously burning, they become heated, from which thermal baths <note>original: *Thermæ*</note> arise; for there are places in the bowels of the earth in which nature [prepares] the mineral sulfur—

...distills and separates the sulfurous substance where it is ignited by the central fire <term>ignis centralis|in early geology and alchemy, a fire believed to exist at the earth's core that powered the growth of minerals and the movement of water</term>. He is the one who, when it is His holy will, can either extinguish the central fire or break the vessel—and that shall be the end of all things.

He will then grant a more powerful intensity to the Central Fire, so that all the waters may be elevated into the air.

# ON SULFUR.

...and the earth will be calcined <term>calcination|the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to remove impurities or reduce it to a fine powder</term>; and thus the fire, having consumed every impurity, will return the subtle, circulated waters from the air to the purified earth, and in this way (if it is permitted to philosophize further) will create a much nobler world.

Therefore, let all seekers of this art <note>The "art" refers to alchemy or natural philosophy</note> know that earth and water form a single globe and act together, for they are the tangible elements in which the other two <note>referring to the "invisible" elements of fire and air</note> operate in secret. Fire preserves the earth so that it is not submerged or dissolved; air preserves the fire so that it is not extinguished; water preserves the earth so that it is not consumed by fire.

It seemed useful to us to describe these things for our purpose, so that students may know upon what foundations the elements rest, and how the Philosophers observed their contrary actions—joining fire with earth, and air with water—though when they sought something noble, they boiled <note>original: "decoxerunt" — to decoct is to extract the essence of something by boiling it down</note> fire in water, noting that one blood is purer than another, just as a tear is purer than urine. Let it suffice you, then, what we have said: that the element of water is the sperm <term>sperm|the generative "seed" or creative life-force from which all things grow</term> and the menstrual fluid <term>menstruum|in alchemy, a solvent or life-giving fluid that dissolves and nurtures the seed</term> of the world, as well as the receptacle of the seed.

# ON THE ELEMENT
### OF AIR.

Air is a complete element, most excellent in its quality; externally it is light and invisible, but internally it is heavy, visible, and fixed <term>fixed|in alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that has been made solid or stable and can no longer be evaporated or changed by fire</term>. It is hot and moist, tempered by fire, and is more noble than earth or water. It is something volatile, yet it can be fixed, and once it is fixed, it makes every body penetrable. 

From its purest substance, the vital spirits <term>vital spirits|the subtle life-force or "breath" believed to animate the bodies of living creatures</term> of animals were created; that which was less pure was raised into the proper Sphere of air, while the remaining coarser part stayed in the water. This coarse part circulates with water just as fire does with earth, because they are "friends" <note>The author refers to alchemical sympathy, where certain elements naturally attract or complement one another.</note>. As we have said, air is most noble, and it is the true location of the seed of all things. Within it, the seed is conceived just as it is in a man, and it is afterwards cast into its sperm by a circular motion. This element possesses the form of integrity to distribute the seed into the matrices <note>original: "matrices" — the wombs or generative environments of nature where things grow</note> through the sperm and the menstrual fluid of the world <term>menstruum of the world|the universal solvent or life-giving fluid that carries the seeds of nature to where they can grow</term>. Within it also exists the vital spirit of every creature, living through all things, penetrating, and binding them together.

# ON THE ELEMENT
### OF FIRE.

Fire is the purest element and the most worthy of all, full of an adhering <term>unctuous corrosivity<gloss>Latin: unctuosae corrosinitatis. In alchemical theory, this refers to a "fatty" or oily quality that allows fire to stick to substances and break them down through corrosion or digestion.</gloss></term>, penetrating, digesting, corroding, and most firmly adhering. It is visible on the outside, but truly invisible within, and is most <term>fixed<gloss>Latin: fixissimum. In alchemy, "fixed" refers to a substance that is stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to heat.</gloss></term>; it is hot and dry, and is tempered by the earth. 

Its most pure substance and essence of all was first raised up with the <term>Throne of Divine Majesty<gloss>Latin: Throno Divinae Maiestatis. This refers to the highest heaven, or Empyrean, which was thought to be composed of the purest celestial fire.</gloss></term> at the creation, when the waters of the heavens were established, as we said in the chapter on the Element of Water. From its less-than-purest substance the Angels were created; and from that which was less pure still, mixed with the purest air, the <term>luminaries<gloss>Latin: luminaria. The "lights" of heaven, specifically the sun, moon, and stars.</gloss></term>

The Most High Creator wished to reveal all natural things to mankind; therefore, He showed us even the heavenly things themselves as natural creations. Through these, His absolute and incomprehensible power and wisdom might be more perfectly understood. All these things the **Philosophers** <note>Early scientists and alchemists who studied the hidden laws of nature.</note> are able to see in the **light of nature** <term>lumen naturae: the spiritual and intellectual insight that allows a person to understand the secrets of the physical world</term>, as if looking into a mirror. 

For this reason, they valued this science highly—not out of a greedy desire for gold or silver, but for the sake of knowledge, not only of all natural things but of the power of the Creator Himself. They chose to speak of these matters as sparingly as possible, and only through figures and allegories, so that the divine mysteries—by which nature is made clear—would not be revealed to the unworthy. You can easily understand this if you know how to know yourself and are not "stiff-necked" <note>A biblical idiom for being stubborn or prideful; original Latin: "durae cervicis"</note>, for you were created in the likeness of the **greater world** <term>macrocosm: the universe as a whole, which was believed to be mirrored in the human body, or "microcosm"</term>, and indeed in the very image of God. ->## ON SULFUR.<-

...for all impurities and pollutions of things are purged by fire. Every composite substance is dissolved by fire; just as water washes and purges all imperfect things that are not fixed <note>In alchemy, "fixed" (*fixae*) refers to substances that are stable in heat and do not evaporate or change state easily; those that remain when others fly away.</note>; so fire purges all fixed things, and through fire they are brought to perfection. Just as water joins together everything that is dissolved, so fire separates everything that is joined; it perfectly purges and increases that which belongs to its own nature and property—not indeed in quantity, but in virtue <note>original: *virtute*; here meaning the essential power, potency, or efficacy of a substance rather than moral virtue.</note>. This Element acts in wonderful ways secretly upon the other Elements and upon all things. For just as the soul is derived from that which is most pure, so the vegetable spirit is derived from the Elemental, which is governed by nature.

This Element acts into the Center of any thing in this way: nature gives motion, motion excites the air, and air excites the fire. Fire, in turn, separates, purges, digests, colors, and causes every seed to ripen; it expels the ripened seed through the sperm <note>original: *sperma*; used here in a broad biological sense to mean reproductive seed or the vital fluid of life.</note> into various places and matrices <note>original: *matrices*; refers to "wombs" or the specific environments within the earth where minerals or plants are generated.</note>. # ON SULPHUR

Separation in the animal kingdom is called death; but since man is also composed and created from the four Elements <note>The four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note>, how could he have been immortal? It is difficult to believe that this could happen naturally; indeed, until now, it has been regarded as something supernatural. However, many centuries ago, God inspired good men, the Philosophers <note>original: "Philosophis"; here referring to "natural philosophers" or alchemists who seek to understand the divine laws of nature.</note>, with the knowledge that this was a natural state, which you should receive from us in this way:

Paradise was and is a place created by the supreme Founder of things from true Elements—not "elementated" <term>elementated (elementatis): things made of the gross, impure, and mixed matter we encounter in the physical world, as opposed to the pure, primordial elements.</term> things, but the purest, tempered elements, perfectly proportioned <gloss>anatisatis: mixed in exact, harmonious balance</gloss> in the highest perfection. Everything that exists in Paradise was created from those same Elements and is incorruptible. There, man was also created, formed from those same incorruptible Elements, constituted with an exact equality of balance so that he could in no way be corrupted. Therefore, he was consecrated to immortality, because God undoubtedly created this paradise for humans alone. We have written extensively about what this [Paradise] is and where it is located in our work, *Harmony* <note>Likely referring to a specific alchemical or philosophical treatise by the author, possibly titled "Harmonia" or "The Harmony of the World."</note>. But when he later transgressed the command of the Most High Creator through the sin of disobedience, [he was cast] into this world...

->### ON SULFUR ###<-

[...and a] disordered life, they hasten toward corruption. Experience teaches this: that those born from the seed of weak parents do not endure. But if man had remained in Paradise, in a place suited to that nature where all the incorrupt Elements are virginal <note>The author uses "virginal" to describe elements in their original, unmixed, and untainted state, as they existed at the moment of Creation.</note>, he would have been immortal for eternity. For it is certain that when pure elements are joined in an equality of their virtues, the resulting subject is incorruptible. Such must be the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>original: lapis Philos.; the "Stone" is not a literal rock, but a metaphorical and physical goal of alchemy—a substance that represents perfectly balanced matter.</note>. To such a human creation, the Stone was likened by the ancient Philosophers; but modern philosophers, taking everything literally, focus instead on the corrupt generation of this current age. 

This immortality was the primary reason why the Philosophers set their minds to seeking this stone, for they knew that man had been created from such whole, unblemished Elements. They meditated, therefore, upon that Creation; and when they realized it was a natural process, they began to investigate whether such incorrupt elements could be obtained, or if they could be joined and infused into some material subject. To these men, the Most High Creator of things inspired the composition of such elements in

# ABOUT SULPHUR.

While they lie in such a confused state, the fire that exists **potentially** <note>original: "potentialiter". In Aristotelian and alchemical thought, a "potential" quality is one that is present but not yet active.</note> within any of the others—such as in the earth and in the air—comes together. Joining forces, they eventually overcome the water, which they digest, cook, and finally congeal. In this manner, nature assists nature. For if the hidden **central fire** <note>original: "ignis centralis"</note>, which was the "captive life" of the substance, prevails, it acts upon what is closest and purest to itself—since it is itself the most pure—and unites with it. Thus, it overcomes its opposite, separating the pure from the impure, and a new form is generated. If it continues to be assisted, this form becomes far more excellent than the previous one; sometimes, through the skill of a wise artisan <note>The "artisan" or "artist" in alchemy usually refers to the alchemist himself, who guides natural processes in the laboratory.</note>, immortal things are created, especially within the **mineral kingdom**. 

Thus, all things are made through fire alone and through the **governance of fire** <note>original: "ignis regimine". In alchemy, the "regimen" or government of fire refers to the careful management of heat intensity and duration during a chemical process.</note>, and are brought into being—provided you have understood me. Here, then, you have the origin of the Elements, their natures, and their operations described as briefly as possible, which is sufficient for our purpose here. For if each Element were described in its full detail, it would produce a massive volume, which is not necessary for our current goal. All these matters, as we have said, we defer to the *Harmony* <note>The author likely refers to another work or a specific section titled "Harmonia."</note>, where, God willing...

# ON THE THREE PRINCIPLES
->of all things.<-

Having described these four Elements, let us descend to the **three principles** <note>These are the *Tria Prima* of alchemy—Sulphur, Mercury, and Salt—which were believed to be the fundamental components of all material substances.</note> of things; as to how they are produced from the four Elements, understand it thus:

After Nature received from the Most High Creator of all things the **feudal privilege** <note>original: "Privilegium feudi". This legal metaphor frames Nature as a vassal who holds authority over the physical world on behalf of God, the supreme Lord.</note> over this **monarchy of the world** <term>Monarchy of the world: a Renaissance concept referring to the total governance and divine order of the physical realm.</term>, she began to distribute places and provinces to each according to their dignity. First, she established the four Elements to be the princes of the world; and so that the will of the Most High (in whose will Nature is placed) might be fulfilled, she ordained that one should act upon another without ceasing. 

Thus, fire began to act upon air, and it produced **Sulphur**. Air also began to act upon water, and it produced **mercury**. Water also began to act upon earth and produced **salt**. Earth, however, having nothing upon which to act, produced nothing; instead, the product remained within it. Therefore, only three principles came forth, and

-># ON SULPHUR #<-

Although the ancient philosophers named only two principles <note>original: "duo tantum principia"</note>, so that the seeker <note>original: "inquisitor," the alchemical researcher</note> does not go astray, it must be known that even though they described **Sulphur** and **Mercury**, they could by no means approach this work without **Salt**. These matters will be explained more fully later in the third treatise on the principles, regarding **Salt**.

Now to the matter at hand: these three **Principles** are entirely necessary, because they are the <term>proximate matter</term> <note>original: "materia propinqua." For the matter of metals is twofold: **Proximate** and **Remote**. The **Proximate** matter is **Sulphur** and **Mercury**; the **Remote** matter consists of the four **Elements**, from which only God is able to create things. 

Leave the **Elements** alone, therefore, because you will make nothing from them, nor will you be able to produce anything except these three **Principles**, since nature herself produces nothing else from them. If, then, you can produce nothing else from the **Elements** but these three principles, why should you engage in this vain labor—wishing to seek or make that which has already been generated by nature? ### ->OF SULPHUR<- ###

external fire does not enter into the depth of the composition essentially, but only through its power. Since the internal material fire <term>internal fire: the innate heat or "sulfur" within a substance that drives its growth</term> is sufficient for itself if it has enough nourishment, and the external fire acts as nourishment for it—much like wood serves an elemental fire—it grows and multiplies according to such nourishment.

One must take care, however, that too much external fire is not applied. For just as a person is choked if they eat too much beyond their capacity, a great flame devours a small fire. The external fire ought to be multiplying and nourishing, not devouring; for that is how things are perfected.

Maturation <note>original: "decoctio," often translated as decoction or "cooking"</note> <term>decoction: a process of prolonged heating to refine, ripen, or extract the essence of a substance</term> is therefore the perfection in all things: in this way, nature adds power to the substance's weight and brings it to completion. But since it is difficult to add anything to a compound—as this requires a very long and tedious labor—we advise that you remove only as much of the excess as is necessary, or as nature requires. 

Once the superfluities <note>meaning the "impurities" or "excesses" that hinder the work</note> have been removed, mix the substance; nature will afterwards reveal what you have been seeking. You will also recognize whether nature has joined the elements well or poorly, for all things depend upon the conjunction of the elements. Yet many who labor...

Common Mercury does not dissolve gold or silver in such a way that it is not separated from them; however, our **quicksilver** dissolves gold and silver and is not separated from them forever, just like water mixed with water. 

Common Mercury contains a bad, combustible <term>sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, "sulphur" represents the internal fire or active principle; "combustible" sulphur was considered an impurity that caused metals to corrode or blacken.</note> by which it is blackened; our **quicksilver** has within it an incombustible, fixed, good, very white, and red sulphur. Common Mercury is cold and moist; our Mercury is hot and moist. Common Mercury blackens and infects bodies; our **quicksilver** whitens bodies even to a crystalline clarity. 

Common Mercury is turned through precipitation into a lemon-yellow powder, a bad sulphur; our **quicksilver**, through the mediation of heat, is turned into a most white, good, fixed, and flowing sulphur. The more common Mercury is boiled down, the more fluid it becomes; the more our **quicksilver** is boiled down, the more in- <note>The text breaks here with the catchword "in-," likely intending to complete the word "induratur" (hardens) or "incrassatur" (thickens) on the following page.</note>

### SIGNATURE H

But to return to the intended subject, we say that **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum," literally "living silver," the common name for mercury.</note> is the primary material of this work, and truly nothing else; whatever is added to it arises from it. We have said several times that all things in the world are created and generated from the **three Principles**; <term>three Principles: the alchemical triad of Sulphur, Mercury, and Salt, believed to be the fundamental components of all matter.</term> but we purge certain things of their "accidents," <note>In alchemical philosophy, "accidents" are external, non-essential impurities or physical states that mask the true, pure essence of a substance.</note> join those purged things together again, add what needs to be added, fill up what is lacking, and—by imitating Nature—we cook them down <note>original: "decoquimus." This refers to the process of "decoction" or "digestion," where substances are subjected to consistent heat to be refined.</note> to the goal of highest perfection. What Nature was unable to complete because of some accident, she has already finished at the point where **Art** <note>In this context, "Art" (Ars) refers specifically to the skilled practice and "science" of alchemy.</note> must begin. 

Therefore, if you desire to imitate Nature, imitate her in those things in which she operates; and do not let it disturb you that our writings seem contrary to one another in some places; for it is necessary that it be so, lest the Art be betrayed. <note>Alchemists often used "Decknamen" (cover names) or contradictory instructions to ensure that only the "worthy" or "initiated" could understand the true process.</note> You, however, must choose those things which agree with Nature: take the roses, leave the thorns; if you intend to make a metal, let metal be your foundation, because nothing is generated from a dog except a dog, and from metal, metal. For know for certain, if you do not take from the metal the **radical moisture** <term>radical moisture: "humidum radicale," a vital, life-sustaining fluid thought to be the fundamental essence of all matter, particularly metals.</term> perfectly separated, you will never achieve anything.

## CONCERNING SULPHUR.

Among the **three Principles** <note>The *Tria Principia* (Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury) were considered by alchemists to be the fundamental building blocks of all material substances.</note>, the **Philosophers** have deservedly placed **Sulphur** in the first rank, as the most worthy Principle, in the preparation of which the entire science lies hidden. For Sulphur is threefold, and must be chosen from among others: Sulphur that **tints** or colors; Sulphur that **congeals** <note>original: *congelans*—referring to the process of turning a liquid substance into a solid state.</note> **Mercury**; and the third is the **essential maturing sulphur**. 

We ought indeed to have treated this subject with great seriousness; but because we have already published one of the principles by way of a **Dialogue**, we are likewise compelled to finish the others in this manner, lest we should seem to do an injustice to any one of them. **Sulphur** is more mature than the other Principles, and **Mercury** is not coagulated <note>To "coagulate" in alchemy often refers to the stabilization or "fixing" of a volatile substance.</note> except by sulphur. Therefore, our total operation in this art is nothing else but knowing how to elicit <note>To "elicit" (draw out) the sulphur from metals was a key goal of the Great Work.</note> sulphur from metals, by which—in the bowels of the earth—our **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*—literally "living silver," the common term for the liquid metal mercury.</note> is congealed into gold and silver. This sulphur indeed takes the place of the **male** in this Work, and is therefore held as more worthy; whereas **Mercury** takes the place of the **female**—

...[generated]. <note>This continues the thought from the previous page regarding the "flies" or delusions produced in the heads of failed alchemists.</note> To speak with <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: *Mercurius*. In alchemy, Mercury represents the principle of volatility, liquidity, and the "spirit" of metals.</note>, I had fished it out from him in a few words: "That other fool," he said, "did not know how to proceed with him." For my part, Mercury never pleased me, nor do I believe there is anything good in him; but <term>Sulphur</term> <note>original: *Sulphur*. The principle of combustion, oiliness, and the "soul" of metals.</note> is approved by me, because in that assembly we disputed most excellently about it; if that storm had not disturbed us, we would have concluded it to be the <term>first matter</term> <note>original: *primam materiam*. The primordial, formless substance that alchemists sought as the starting point for the Great Work.</note>. Since my head is not accustomed to light things, my head is full of profound imaginations. 

And so, confirmed in this belief, he resolved to work on sulphur. He began, therefore, to <term>distill</term>, <term>sublimate</term>, <term>calcine</term> <note>original: *calcinare*. Heating a substance to high temperatures in air to achieve oxidation or reduction to powder.</note>, and <term>roast</term> <note>original: *frixare*. Literally "to fry," referring here to intense heating in a pan.</note> it. He attempted to make <term>oil by the bell</term> <note>original: *oleum per campanam*. A method of producing sulfuric acid by burning sulfur under a glass bell jar and collecting the resulting acidic moisture.</note> from it, sometimes by itself, and sometimes with crystals, <term>eggshells</term> <note>original: *corticibus ouorum*. A common source of calcium carbonate in early chemistry.</note>, and he attempted many other labors upon it. 

When he had consumed much time and expense, and could find nothing suited to his purpose, he was saddened. The wretch spent many sleepless nights; he even went out frequently outside the city to contemplate, so that he might more conveniently think of something certain in his labor. ...while he was walking about in an ecstatic contemplation <term>Ecstasy (extasi): In this context, a state of meditative trance or a vision where the alchemist's mind wanders through symbolic realms rather than the physical world.</term>, he came to a certain very green grove, which was most filled with all things; in which there were mines of all minerals and metals, and kinds of all animals and birds, and an abundance of all trees, herbs, and fruits. There were also various aqueducts there, since in those places water was not to be had, except that which was drawn through various instruments and channels; and this was done by various craftsmen from various places. The chief of these was clearer than the rest, which was drawn from the rays of the Moon <note>original: "radijs Lunae." In alchemy, "lunar water" or moisture drawn from the moon often symbolizes the "white" stage of the work or a specific celestial influence required for the transformation of matter.</note>; and this was provided only for the Nymph of that grove. Bulls and rams were also grazing there, and the shepherds were two youths, whom the Alchemist questioned, saying: "Whose forest is this?" To which they replied: "It is the grove and garden of our Nymph, Venus." <note>In the symbolic language of alchemy, Venus represents both the goddess of beauty and the metal copper. Her garden is a frequent allegory for the place where the "seeds" of metals grow.</note> The Alchemist walked here and there. <margin>H 5</margin>

...having spent his labor in vain (he was not otherwise a man of deceptive skill <note>original: "deceptorię facultatis." This indicates he was not a "sophist" or a con-artist charlatan, but a sincere, if misguided, seeker.</note>, but rather harmful to himself) and said: "What is this? Everyone says the matter is common, cheap, and easy, and yet I am a learned man, but I cannot figure out this wretched stone." And so, lamenting in this way, he began to curse sulphur, because he had wasted so much expense and so much labor on it in vain. And sulphur was indeed in that grove, but the Alchemist was unaware of it. 

Meanwhile, while he was thus lamenting, he heard a voice as if from some old man: "Friend, why do you lament so? Why do you curse sulphur?" The Alchemist looked around everywhere, and seeing no one, he was terrified. But that voice said again: "Friend, why are you so saddened?" 

The Alchemist, having regained his courage, said: "Lord, just as a hungry man always thinks of bread, so I always think of the Philosophers' Stone <term>Lapide Philo. (Lapide Philosophorum): The legendary substance sought by alchemists, capable of transmuting base metals into gold and acting as a universal medicine.</term>." 

*Voice:* "And why do you curse sulphur?" 

*Alchemist:* "Lord, I believed it to be the first matter <term>prima materia: The original, primal substance from which all things are created, and the necessary starting point for the Alchemical Work.</term> of the Philosophers' Stone; therefore, by working on it for several years, I have consumed much, and I have not been able to find that Stone."

*Alch.* And why is it imprisoned?

*Vox:* Because it wished to be obedient to all Alchemists, and to do whatever they themselves desired, against the will of its mother. She had forbidden it, commanding that it should obey only those who truly knew her; therefore, she delivered it into prison, ordered its feet to be bound, and appointed guards for it, so that it could go nowhere without their knowledge and consent.

*Alch.* Oh, wretched thing! For that reason it could not help me either. Certainly its mother does it a great injury! And when will it be released from those prisons?

*Alchemist:* Lord, does Sulphur also know anything within the metals?

*Voice:* I have told you that it knows everything, and in metals much better than elsewhere; but its guards know that there it can easily be set free, therefore they keep it detained there in the harshest of prisons <note>In alchemical allegory, "prisons" or "cells" typically refer to the base matter or the "crust" of mineral ores that hold the active chemical principle captive.</note>, so that it cannot even breathe; for they fear lest it should arrive at the King’s palace <note>The "King's palace" represents the state of chemical perfection or the completed "Great Work," where the substance is transformed into gold.</note>.

*Alchemist:* Lord, is it thus imprisoned in all metals?

*Voice:* In all, but not uniformly; in some, it is not held so strictly.

*Alchemist:* Lord, and why in metals is it treated in such a tyrannical fashion?

*Voice:* Because it would no longer fear them once it has reached its Royal palace: for now it can be seen, now it gazes freely from the windows; since it is there, in its own Kingdom, though not yet as it desires.

*Alchemist:* Lord, and what does it eat?

*Voice:* Its food is the wind <term>Wind: Often referred to as "pneuma" or "spiritus," the wind was believed by alchemists to be the vehicle that carried the vital life-force or "food" of the soul of a substance.</term>: when it is free, the food is cooked; but in its prisons, it is forced to eat it raw.

*Alchemist:* Lord, can these enmities between...

## CONCERNING SULPHUR.

They did exactly as the Alchemist wished, and they began to work that sulphur in strange ways, treating it in wonderful furnaces, of which the Alchemist had many; but always in the end, from every labor, there resulted for them only those little candles <note>original: *candelulæ*; these were small sulfur-dipped sticks used by common people as matches to start fires, representing a mundane rather than a spiritual or transmutative result</note> which old women commonly sell for lighting a fire. For whatever the Alchemist wanted from that sulphur, it always remained a little candle. And he said to Saturn, **"Truly Lord, I see that it will not succeed according to my imagination; I beg you, do it yourself as you know how."** Then Saturn said, **"Watch, therefore, and learn."** And so he took two types of quicksilver <note>original: *argenta viua*; literally "living silvers," the alchemical name for Mercury</note> of different substance but of one root, which Saturn washed with his own urine <note>In alchemical texts, "Saturn's urine" is a metaphorical term usually referring to a specific distilled solvent or a solution of lead salts used in the purification process</note>, and he called those the "sulphurs of sulphurs." He mixed the fixed with the volatile, and once the composition was made, he placed it in a proper vessel. And lest the sulphur should flee, he placed a guard <note>original: *custodem*; likely a reference to hermetically sealing the vessel to prevent the escape of vapors</note> over it, and afterward he placed it in a bath of the slowest fire <note>original: *balneum ignis lentissimi*; a gentle, consistent heat source, like a water or sand bath</note>, as the material required, and he did everything perfectly. Thus they made the 
->Philosophers'<- 
Stone.

## CONCERNING SULPHUR.

I

...Philosophical <note>Continuing from the previous page: "and once the mixture was complete, the result was..."</note>, because from the proper material, only the proper work results. The Alchemist, greatly rejoiced, took the stone together with the glass vessel; contemplating its color, which was like **burnt blood** <note>original: *sanguis combustus*; in alchemy, a deep red color often signifies the final stage of the Great Work, known as the *rubedo*</note>, he was filled with wonder. Out of excessive joy, he began to dance, and during this dance, the glass vessel slipped from his hands to the ground and shattered; and thus Saturn vanished.

The Alchemist also woke up from his dream and found nothing in his hands except those little candles <note>original: *candelulas*; as established previously, these represent mundane matches rather than alchemical gold</note> which he had made from sulphur. The stone, however, flew away, and it flies to this day—which is why it is called *volatile*—and so that wretched Alchemist learned nothing else from that vision than how to make sulfurous candles. Afterwards, having abandoned the stone, he began to be a Doctor; while searching for the **Philosophers' Stone**, he acquired instead a **kidney stone** <note>original: *lapidem renum*; a play on words where the seeker finds a painful medical ailment instead of the miraculous elixir</note>.

Finally, he led the kind of life such Alchemists usually do; they mostly become doctors or **ointment-makers** <term>smegmatistae: from the Greek word for soap or unguent; here referring to those who pivot from failed alchemy to selling common cosmetics or salves</term>. This is what happens to all those who approach the art without a foundation, relying only on hearsay or chance recipes and logical arguments. Some of them afterwards, when they do not succeed, still say: **"We are wise men."**

# CONCERNING SULPHUR.

...we hear the grass grow <note>original: "gramen crefcere audimus"; likely a proverbial expression for those who claim to notice or know things that are impossible or hidden.</note>; if the <term>true art</term> <note>original: "ars vera"</note> were real, we would have possessed it before all others. And so, clothed in shame—lest they be considered unworthy (as they indeed are, being moreover "stiff-necked")—they "cuckoo" against the art <note>The author uses the verb "cuculant" to suggest these critics chatter mindlessly or mockingly like cuckoo birds.</note>. This science hates such people, and always shows them the beginning at the end <note>A phrase suggesting that these critics are stuck in a loop, never making actual progress because they lack the proper spirit.</note>. 

We concede to the unworthy that this art is nothing; but to the lovers of virtue, the true seekers, and the <term>sons of doctrine</term> <note>original: "filijfque doctrinæ"; a traditional alchemical term referring to students or initiates into the "Great Work."</note>, we highly commend it. We affirm it to be true and altogether most certain, for we have proven it by the work itself several times before men worthy of such a sight, both of high and low social standing. 

However, this <term>medicine</term> <note>original: "medicina"; in alchemical texts, the "Medicine" usually refers to the Philosopher's Stone, which was believed to "heal" base metals into gold or cure human illness.</note> was not made by our own hands; rather, we received such things from a friend. They are nonetheless most true, and we have provided sufficient instruction to seekers for their discovery. If our writings do not please them, let them read the lighter works of other authors, but with this caution: that whatever they read, they must always compare it to the possibility of <term>nature</term>. They should not attempt anything against nature, nor should they believe—even if it were written in the books of the <term>Philosophers</term>—that <term>fire</term> does not burn.

<summary>The author defends the reality of alchemy against arrogant critics, asserting that while the "unworthy" find nothing in it, "sons of doctrine" can find the truth if they follow the laws of nature and common sense.</summary>

# CONCERNING SULPHUR.

I 2

...burns, because this is contrary to nature; but if it is written that fire has the power of drying and heating, this is to be believed, since it happens naturally. For nature always agrees with sound intellect, and in nature nothing is difficult, and every truth is simple. 

Then let them also learn to recognize which things in nature are more closely related to one another; we believe this is achieved more easily through our writings than through others, as we think we have written enough—at least until someone else comes along who can write down the whole recipe as clearly as one might describe making cheese from milk <note>A metaphor for a simple, natural transformation that is easy to understand but requires specific steps.</note>, which we are not permitted to do <note>Alchemists often claimed they were divinely or philosophically forbidden from being too explicit to prevent the unworthy from abusing the art.</note>. 

But lest we direct everything toward beginners, we will also say something to you who have already passed through these labors <note>original: "ærumnas"; refers to the difficult and often frustrating physical work of the laboratory.</note>. 

**HAVE YOU SEEN** that region where the man led his wife to himself, whose marriage was celebrated in the house of nature? Did you understand how the common people saw this <term>sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, "sulphur" often represents the active, masculine, or combustible principle of a substance.</note> along with you? If therefore you wish for old women to practice your <term>philosophy</term> <note>The "Art" or "Science" of alchemy was frequently called "Philosophy."</note>, show the <term>whitening</term> <note>original: "dealbatio"; also known as the "albedo" stage, where the matter is purified and turns white.</note> of these sulphurs. Say to the common people: "Come and see, for now the water is divided and the sulphur has gone out; it will return white, and it will congeal the waters."

# CONCERNING SULPHUR.

Burn away, therefore, the [common] sulphur by means of the incombustible sulphur, <note>original: "sulphur à fulphure incombustibili"; the "incombustible sulphur" is the fixed, internal fire of the philosophers that purifies the "vulgar" or combustible sulphur.</note> and wash it, whiten it, and redden it, <note>These represent the stages of the Great Work: *albedo* (whitening) and *rubedo* (reddening).</note> until the sulphur becomes mercury, and the mercury becomes sulphur, which you will afterwards adorn with the soul of gold. <note>original: "anima auri"; the spiritual essence or coloring principle extracted from gold to "fix" the medicine.</note> For if you do not sublimate the sulphur from the sulphur, and the mercury from the mercury, you have not yet found that water which is created and distilled from sulphur and mercury as the quintessence; <note>original: "quinta essentia"; the "fifth essence," a pure, celestial force extracted from matter.</note> he who did not descend shall not ascend. <note>A variation of the Hermetic principle found in the Emerald Tablet, implying a cycle of evaporation and condensation.</note> Whatever is noteworthy in this art is lost by many during the preparation; for our mercury is sharpened by the sulphur, otherwise it would be of no use. A prince without a people is a wretched thing; such is the alchemist without sulphur and mercury. If you have understood me, I have said enough.
